# HPC / AI Fault & Failure Dataset Catalog

**Compiled:** 2026-06-25, from an exhaustive multi-agent search (12 discovery angles mining failure-analysis and AI-training papers plus the dataset repositories, then per-dataset enrichment).
**For:** the baseline fault-characterization study Dr. Bangalore asked for on 2026-06-25 — characterize real HPC vs AI-workload failures before building any predictor or mitigation.
**Count:** **162 entries** (118 from the main search + 44 added by the completeness pass). Every entry carries exactly one evidence verdict in [`ledger.csv`](ledger.csv). *(Corrected 2026-09-20: an earlier header said "~157", which added full records to prose-bullet mentions. The catalog has always held 118 main-body entries; the additions block now holds 44, all structured entries.)* The field that matters most is **Failure info** — what fault signal each set actually exposes. Many carry only telemetry or job status, not labeled causes.

> Provenance: the first 118 came from a search run that hit a session limit before its final auto-synthesis (so a few near-duplicate entries remain and some are flagged low-confidence). A follow-up completeness pass then added the datasets the interrupted run had missed — those are collected in the **Additions from completeness critic** section at the end, including the highest-value one (SURF Lisa). Verify access links before citing.

> **Read the Additions section.** Several of the most on-point datasets for the AI-vs-HPC question (SURF Lisa, NREL Eagle/Kestrel, ALCF Cobalt+RAS, Unicron, L4) are there, not in the main body.

---

## Shortlist: which datasets can answer "do AI workloads fail more than HPC, and why?"

The study's core question. Prioritize sets with **labeled job outcomes or failure causes**, not just telemetry.

| Dataset | Why it fits | Side |
|---|---|---|
| **SURF Lisa "Generic and ML Workloads"** ⭐ | Generic-HPC and ML jobs on ONE machine, ~10 mo Slurm traces + node telemetry, per-job exit state + energy-wasted-on-failure; explicitly splits ML vs generic. The most direct answer to the question. Public (Zenodo). *(see Additions)* | both |
| **AcmeTrace (Seren + Kalos)** | LLM-training traces with utilization + a failure/RAS subset; basis for the Acme NSDI'24 ~40%-job-failure finding | AI |
| **Meta "Revisiting Reliability in Large-Scale ML Research Clusters"** | Direct AI-side failure rates and causes from Meta research clusters | AI |
| **Unicron / L4 / Meta FT-HSDP** | Published normalized AI failure rates: Unicron 43% job-failure (hardware-dominated); L4 428 real LLM failures with root causes; Meta 2.3 interruptions per 1k servers/day, 78% hardware. *(see Additions)* | AI |
| **Alibaba GPU trace 2023 (+2020)** | Modern production GPU cluster with job status; two years give a trend | AI |
| **Llama-3 405B interruption breakdown** | Root-cause table for every interruption in a 405B run | AI |
| **NREL Eagle / Kestrel** | 11M+ Slurm jobs each, clean exit states (FAILED/NODE_FAIL/TIMEOUT/OOM); large generic-HPC failure-rate baseline. Public. *(see Additions)* | HPC |
| **ALCF Cobalt + RAS traces** | Job termination info tied to separate RAS/hardware-fault logs, a rare job-outcome-to-node-fault pairing. Public. *(see Additions)* | HPC |
| **F-DATA (Fugaku)** | Job-centric dataset built for predictive modeling, includes exit/failure status, Tier-0 CPU side | HPC |
| **M100 ExaData (Marconi100)** | 49.9 TB / 934 days telemetry + SLURM + Nagios alarms; ready anomaly sub-dataset | HPC |
| **Mira 2K-day multi-source job failure logs** | RAS + Cobalt + task-execution joined; rare labeled-cause HPC set | HPC |
| **Blue Waters (LogDiver)** | Classic large-scale HPC failure/error characterization | HPC |

Honest catch (Bangalore flagged it): HPC job traces mostly give exit codes that get overwritten on kill and lack app/Slurm context, so *cause* is often missing. The AI-side reports (Llama-3, OPT-175B, MegaScale, Meta) have hand-written causes but small samples. A credible baseline likely joins a telemetry set (M100) with a labeled-cause set (Mira multi-source, F-DATA) plus the AI narratives.

## Datasets Bangalore & Hari named specifically

- **RoWD / "Rowdy"** = the **Rogue Workload Detector** framework, which releases the **SCRIPT-AI** dataset. Almost certainly the framework Bangalore pointed at ("available scripts"). Confirm with him/Hari and get script access. See *Other / repositories & frameworks*.
- **Tachyon** = KISTI Tachyon-II scheduler job failure records (under *Job / scheduler / workload traces*).
- **ALCF / Polaris / Theta / Argonne ("Aragon")** = ALCF Public Data Catalog + Polaris/Theta Darshan and job collections. The ~25-30% Darshan coverage Bangalore mentioned makes these sample-based.
- **Hari's combined data** = not public; get it from Hari directly (you lead the fault analysis, he's potential co-author).

## AI / GPU-training failures & traces  (19)

### Acme LLM cluster trace (Shanghai AI Lab / AcmeTrace)
*aka:* Acme; AcmeTrace; LLM development trace; Seren/Kalos trace
*Org:* Shanghai AI Laboratory (InternLM team); analysis co-authored with SJTU (Tianwei Zhang et al.)  
*System:* Datacenter "Acme" — two A100 GPU clusters: Seren and Kalos  
*Period:* 6 months, March–August 2023  
*Scale:* 4,704 NVIDIA A100 GPUs total across the two clusters. Seren: ~368K CPU jobs + 664K GPU jobs; Kalos: ~42K CPU jobs + 20K GPU jobs. Aggregate in the released trac
- **Contents:** Scheduler job traces (CSV: job_id, user, node_num, gpu_num, cpu_num, mem_per_pod_GB, shared_mem_per_pod, type, state, submit_time, start_time, end_time, fail_time, stop_time, duration, queue, gpu_time), plus infrastructure/resource utilization monitoring from DCGM, IPMI and Prometheus (CPU util, DRAM activity, GPU power/utilization/temperature). Repo ships job_trace/ (trace_seren.csv, trace_kalos.csv), utilization/, cluster_summary.csv, and analysis Jupyter notebooks + utils.py. This is an LLM-development-specific trace (pretraining, evaluation, debugging jobs), not a generic DL trace.
- **Failure info:** PARTIAL — split between the released trace and the paper. The released CSVs expose job OUTCOME STATES only: COMPLETED, CANCELLED, FAILED, TIMEOUT, NODE_FAIL (docs note TIMEOUT/NODE_FAIL are rare and counted as failed), plus failure/stop TIMING via fail_time and stop_time (stop/fail-time columns populated notably on Kalos). The public trace does NOT contain per-job root-cause labels, exit codes, or error messages. The dedicated FAILURE TAXONOMY with root causes lives in the PAPER (Table 3), not as a field in the CSV: 3 top-level categories — Infrastructure, Framework, Script — each with specific error reasons: Infrastructure = NVLinkError, CUDAError, NodeFailure, ECCError, NetworkError, Conne
- **Access:** Public. GitHub: https://github.com/InternLM/AcmeTrace (license CC-BY-4.0). Full ~80GB processed dataset hosted on HuggingFace (linked from repo). Paper open-access: USENIX NSDI '24 https://www.usenix.org/conference/nsdi24/presentation/hu and arXiv:2403.07648
- **Used by:** Hu et al., "Characterization of Large Language Model Development in the Datacenter," USENIX NSDI 2024 (arXiv:2403.07648) — the origin paper. Widely reused as a workload/failure reference in subsequent GPU-cluster scheduling and fault-tolerance work (e.g., MoE inference resilience "Tarragon" arXiv:26
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (LLM development: pretraining, evaluation, debugging on GPU clusters). Highly relevant to "do AI workloads fail more and why": it quantifies that failures are dominated by long-running large-model pretraining jobs, that Infrastructure failures (NVLink/CUDA/ECC/network) consume ~82%+ of waste
- **Caveats:** Failure taxonomy (root causes, error types) is in the PAPER's aggregate Table 3, not as a labeled column in the public trace — the CSVs give only coarse job states (FAILED/CANCELLED/TIMEOUT/NODE_FAIL) and fail/stop timestamps; no exit codes or raw error logs are released (privacy/proprietary). fail_time/stop_time populated mainly for Kalos. Root-cause labeling in the paper relied partly on GPT-4-a

### AcmeTrace (Seren + Kalos cluster traces)
*aka:* Acme trace; InternLM/AcmeTrace; Qinghao/AcmeTrace
*Org:* Shanghai AI Laboratory (InternLM team)  
*System:* Acme datacenter — two clusters, Seren and Kalos  
*Period:* 6 months, March–August 2023  
*Scale:* 4,704 NVIDIA A100 GPUs total across Seren + Kalos; 880,740 total jobs, of which 470,497 are GPU jobs; ~80 GB full release (109 MB core on GitHub)
- **Contents:** Three data layers. (1) Scheduler/job trace (trace_seren.csv, trace_kalos.csv): job_id, user, node_num, gpu_num, cpu_num, submit/start/end timestamps, duration, queue time, gpu_time (duration x gpu_num), state; Kalos adds memory config, fail_time, and stop_time fields. (2) Resource utilization at 15-second sampling: CPU util (Prometheus), GPU metrics (DCGM), and node power consumption (IPMI) — stored per-cluster plus processed util_pkl pickle files. (3) A cluster_summary.csv comparing Seren/Kalos alongside Philly/Helios/PAI aggregate stats (incl. fail_rate_gpu/cpu, complete_rate, queue-time medians). Analysis Jupyter notebook and figure examples included.
- **Failure info:** Partial. The released job trace DOES label terminal job outcomes via the Slurm-style `state` field with values COMPLETED, CANCELLED, FAILED, TIMEOUT, NODE_FAIL — so failed vs. node-failed vs. timed-out jobs are distinguishable, and Kalos additionally exposes a `fail_time` timestamp for when failure occurred. However, the public trace does NOT provide per-job exit codes, error messages, or root-cause/error-category labels. The paper's richer failure taxonomy — three root-cause classes Infrastructure (compute/storage hardware, mid-run, dominant in pretraining), Framework (RuntimeError/ValueError/AttributeError tensor-op errors, early in run), and Script (user/programming errors, the majority) 
- **Access:** Public. Core files (~109 MB) at https://github.com/InternLM/AcmeTrace ; full ~80 GB set at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Qinghao/AcmeTrace (note: HF page currently shows a DatasetGenerationCastError from mismatched CSV schemas in the viewer, but files are downloadable). License CC-BY-4.0.
- **Used by:** Hu et al., "Characterization of Large Language Model Development in the Datacenter," USENIX NSDI 2024 (arXiv:2403.07648) — the originating flagship paper. Widely cited as a reference LLM-training trace in subsequent GPU-cluster reliability, scheduling, and failure-characterization work.
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (dedicated LLM development/pretraining clusters, all A100 GPUs). Directly relevant to the "do AI workloads fail, and how" question: it quantifies failure rates and outcome states for real large-scale LLM training and, via the companion paper, characterizes why AI jobs fail (long-running pret
- **Caveats:** The 80 GB HuggingFace mirror's dataset viewer is broken (schema cast error from mixing 17-column job rows with 28-column aggregate rows) though files remain downloadable. Root-cause failure labels (Infrastructure/Framework/Script) are the most valuable failure signal but are a paper-level analysis, not verified as a released per-job labeled file — treat root cause as ABSENT/ambiguous in the raw tr
- **Confidence:** high — dataset existence, clusters, scal

### Alibaba 2023 GPU cluster trace (cluster-trace-gpu-v2023) + HPN link/switch failure statistics
*aka:* Alibaba PAI GPU trace 2023; cluster-trace-gpu-v2023; openb trace; Alibaba HPN failure rates
*Org:* Alibaba (Alibaba Cloud / PAI Platform for AI; HPN from Alibaba Cloud networking team)  
*System:* Alibaba PAI production heterogeneous GPU cluster (trace); Alibaba HPN LLM-training datacenter network (HPN paper)  
*Period:* Trace: a production snapshot in 2023 (exact calendar window not disclosed in README). HPN paper: SIGCOMM 2024, cites ~8 months of production/operational experie  
*Scale:* Trace: 1,523 nodes (~1,200 GPU machines, 6,200+ GPUs), 8,152 tasks/pods; GPU types G1/G2/G3 + V100 variants. HPN: ~15,000 GPUs, dual-ToR, 8 months of reported o
- **Contents:** Trace (openb_pod_list + node CSVs): per-pod CPU (milli), memory (MiB), GPU count, fractional GPU-milli for shared workloads, GPU-type constraint, QoS level, pod phase (Pending/Running/Succeeded/Failed), and creation/scheduled/deletion timestamps; per-node CPU/mem/GPU capacity and GPU model. Built for a Kubernetes scheduler simulator (Fragmentation Gradient Descent work). HPN paper: prose/aggregate network-reliability statistics only, no per-event records.
- **Failure info:** CRITICAL DISTINCTION — the seed conflates two things. (1) The PUBLIC clusterdata trace (cluster-trace-gpu-v2023) is scheduling/telemetry ONLY. It has NO fault labels, NO exit codes, NO error codes, NO root cause. The single failure-adjacent signal is the Kubernetes pod phase field, which can be 'Failed' — but that is an unqualified terminal state with no reason, no exit code, and no distinction between hardware fault, OOM, preemption, or user error. Effectively not a failure dataset. (2) The Alibaba HPN paper (SIGCOMM 2024) reports AGGREGATE network-failure RATES in text: ~0.057% of NIC-ToR links fail per month, ~0.051% of ToR switches hit critical error/crash per month, 1-2 training-job cra
- **Access:** Public, open. Trace: https://github.com/alibaba/clusterdata/tree/master/cluster-trace-gpu-v2023 . HPN paper (open PDF): https://ennanzhai.github.io/pub/sigcomm24-hpn.pdf and dl.acm.org SIGCOMM '24. No login or request needed; repo has no explicit license file called out in the 2023 README.
- **Used by:** 'GPU Cluster Dynamics: Insights from Alibaba's 2023 Trace Release', Computing (Springer) 2024, doi:10.1007/s00607-024-01369-9 (the seed paper; analyzes the trace). 'Beware of Fragmentation: Scheduling GPU-Sharing Workloads with Fragmentation Gradient Descent', USENIX ATC '23 (origin of the trace/sim
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (both artifacts are AI/LLM-GPU workloads: PAI ML-as-a-Service scheduling + LLM-training network). LIMITED help for 'do AI workloads fail more than HPC and why': the public trace has no root-caused failures to compare against HPC logs, and the HPN numbers are network-link/switch failure RATES
- **Caveats:** Seed merged two separate deliverables under one name — worth splitting in any catalog. The public trace is a scheduler/fragmentation trace, not a fault dataset; its only failure signal (pod phase = Failed) lacks any cause or exit code. The HPN paper releases NO dataset — its failure percentages are aggregate text statistics, easily mis-cited as a downloadable trace. Trace calendar window and licen
- **Confidence:** high — both artifacts confirmed to exist

### Alibaba GPU Cluster Trace 2020 (cluster-trace-gpu-v2020)
*aka:* Alibaba PAI GPU trace 2020; MLaaS trace; MLaaS-in-the-Wild trace
*Org:* Alibaba (Alibaba Group / Alibaba Cloud PAI)  
*System:* Alibaba PAI (Platform for Artificial Intelligence) production GPU cluster / MLaaS  
*Period:* July-August 2020 (2 months)  
*Scale:* >6,500 GPUs on ~1,800 heterogeneous machines; hundreds of thousands of jobs/tasks/instances (exact totals not stated in README). Multiple GPU types (fractional/
- **Contents:** Seven CSV tables: pai_job_table (job_name, inst_id, user, status, start_time, end_time), pai_task_table (job_name, task_name, inst_num, status, start_time, end_time, plan_cpu, plan_mem, plan_gpu, gpu_type), pai_instance_table (job_name, task_name, inst_name, worker_name, inst_id, status, start_time, end_time, machine), pai_sensor_table (per-instance resource-utilization sensors: CPU/GPU/mem/IO), pai_group_tag_table (workload/semantic tags, GPU type, group), pai_machine_spec (per-machine CPU cores, memory, GPU count), pai_machine_metric (machine-level aggregate metrics over instance lifespan). Jobs decompose into tasks by role (PS + worker for training, evaluator for inference).
- **Failure info:** PARTIAL / LABEL-ONLY. Each job, task, and instance carries a categorical status field with values Running, Terminated, Failed, Waiting — where only "Terminated" means successful completion, so "Failed" is an explicit failure/unsuccessful-job label (and non-terminated states also capture cancelled/incomplete work). This lets you count and time-locate failed ML jobs and correlate failure with GPU type, allocation, task role (PS/worker), and resource usage. CRITICAL LIMITATION: the status is the ONLY failure signal. There are NO exit codes, NO error messages, NO fault/error categories, and NO root-cause information (hardware fault vs OOM vs user bug vs preemption vs eviction are all indistingui
- **Access:** Public. GitHub: https://github.com/alibaba/clusterdata/tree/master/cluster-trace-gpu-v2020 (README + schema + download links); data files via Alibaba OSS links and a GitHub alternative mirror. Also mirrored on Kaggle (kaggle.com/datasets/derrickmwiti/cluster-trace-gpu-v2020). No explicit OSS license stated in the README; use governed by the Alibaba clusterdata program terms.
- **Used by:** Weng et al., "MLaaS in the Wild: Workload Analysis and Scheduling in Large-Scale Heterogeneous GPU Clusters," NSDI 2022 (the defining/characterization paper). Widely reused in GPU-cluster scheduling, GPU-sharing/fractional-allocation, and workload-characterization research building on that trace.
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only. This is real production ML/DL (training + inference) workload on GPUs, not HPC/MPI batch. It CAN partly inform "do AI workloads fail?" — it gives an AI-workload failure RATE via the Failed status and lets you relate failure to GPU sharing, job structure, and utilization. But it CANNOT answe
- **Caveats:** Dataset existence and field schema verified directly from the Alibaba clusterdata GitHub README. Gaps/label noise: (1) No failure cause, exit code, or error text — "Failed" is opaque and conflates hardware faults, OOM, user code errors, preemption, and eviction. (2) Non-"Terminated" statuses (Failed/Running/Waiting) blur the line between genuine failures and cancelled/still-running/queued work; a 

### Alibaba GPU cluster trace 2023 (cluster-trace-gpu-v2023)
*aka:* Alibaba PAI GPU trace 2023; Alibaba Kubernetes Scheduler Simulator openb trace; "Fragmentation" trace
*Org:* Alibaba (Alibaba Cloud / PAI platform, with HKUST co-authors)  
*System:* Alibaba production heterogeneous GPU cluster (PAI platform), traces packaged as the "openb" cluster for the Kubernetes Scheduler Simulator  
*Period:* 2023 release; underlying window not explicitly dated in the docs (timestamps given in seconds: creation, scheduled, deletion). Effectively a workload snapshot r  
*Scale:* 1,523 nodes / heterogeneous GPU cluster; >6,200 GPUs on ~1,200 machines; 8,152 tasks (pods) submitted. Node records list CPU (milli-cores), memory (MiB), GPU co
- **Contents:** Two main tables. Node/machine table: CPU (milli-cores), main memory (MiB), GPU count, GPU model/type. Task/pod table: CPU/memory/GPU resource requests, fractional GPU-sharing spec (GPU milli), GPU-type constraint, QoS class (Burstable / Best Effort / Latency Sensitive), pod phase (Succeeded / Running / Pending / Failed), and creation/scheduled/deletion timestamps. Shipped as CSV plus YAML, with conversion scripts (e.g., pod_csv_to_yaml.py) to drive the Kubernetes Scheduler Simulator. Purpose-built to study GPU-sharing scheduling and GPU fragmentation.
- **Failure info:** Effectively NONE for fault analysis. The only failure-adjacent field is the Kubernetes pod "phase," whose values include "Failed" alongside Succeeded/Running/Pending. That is a coarse lifecycle/scheduling status, NOT a fault record: there are no exit codes, no error/signal codes, no termination reason, no root-cause or failure-category labels, and no repair/restart events. "Failed" here largely reflects scheduling/admission or pod-lifecycle outcome, not hardware/software fault diagnosis. The trace is resource-request and scheduling telemetry aimed at fragmentation, so it cannot support failure-cause or reliability analysis.
- **Access:** Public, open access on GitHub: https://github.com/alibaba/clusterdata/tree/master/cluster-trace-gpu-v2023 (README: .../blob/master/cluster-trace-gpu-v2023/README.md). Part of the broader Alibaba Cluster Trace Program.
- **Used by:** Weng et al., "Beware of Fragmentation: Scheduling GPU-Sharing Workloads with Fragmentation Gradient Descent" (USENIX ATC 2023) — the originating paper and Kubernetes Scheduler Simulator (FGD vs Best-fit/Dot-product/GPU-Packing/GPU-Clustering/Random-fit baselines). Also analyzed in "GPU cluster dynam
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (GPU deep-learning/ML training and inference workloads on a Kubernetes-managed GPU cluster; no traditional MPI/HPC batch jobs). It does NOT help answer "do AI workloads fail more than HPC and why": it has no comparable HPC counterpart in the same trace and, more importantly, no genuine failu
- **Caveats:** The seed's "AI-GPU-training-failures" framing is misleading: this is a fragmentation/scheduling trace, not a failure trace. Exact calendar time window and duration are not clearly documented. GPU types are given as anonymized internal codes (G1/G2/G3) rather than full model names in some fields. The "Failed" pod phase is ambiguous (scheduling/lifecycle outcome, not diagnosed fault) and should not 
- **Confidence:** high — dataset existence, scale, content

- **Caveats:** Failure signal is thin: 'Failed' status exists only in v2020 and is a coarse outcome label with no cause, exit code, or fault type; distinguishing user-cancellation from system/hardware failure is not possible. v2023 and Lingjun-2023 have NO failure labels at all (telemetry/scheduling/topology only). ### Fire-Flyer 2 AI-HPC Xid Error Characterization (DeepSeek)
*aka:* DeepSeek Fire-Flyer GPU failure appendix; Fire-Flyer 2 Xid error breakdown; An et al. SC'24 hardware-failure characterization
*Org:* DeepSeek AI / High-Flyer (Hangzhou/Beijing, China)  
*System:* DeepSeek Fire-Flyer 2 AI-HPC  
*Period:* Roughly one year of operational data, spanning 2023 to 2024 (Fig. 10 "Trends of Memory and Network Failures from 2023 to 2024").  
*Scale:* ~10,000 NVIDIA PCIe A100 GPUs; ~1,250 GPU compute nodes; ~200 storage servers. PCIe-based (NVLink Bridge, not NVSwitch), 8 GPUs/node, 200Gbps IB.
- **Contents:** Aggregate GPU Xid-error statistics from the production cluster: a categorization of Xid error types and their causes (Table V) plus a percentage breakdown of observed Xid errors by class, and a time-trend figure of memory vs. network failures. Also narrative on the checkpoint/recovery system (async checkpoint to 3FS, ~5 min recovery). This is telemetry-derived error accounting, not raw per-job logs.
- **Failure info:** CRITICAL FIELD: This DOES label failures by fault class — it uses NVIDIA Xid error codes as the fault taxonomy, which is genuine root-cause categorization at the hardware-signal level (not just raw telemetry). Reported distribution: Xid 74 (NVLink errors) = 42.57% (attributed to inherent fault rate of NVLink Bridge connectors amplified at scale); Xid 43 (illegal/out-of-bounds memory access, software/user-code) = 33.48%; software-related codes Xid 13/31/43/45 grouped as software errors; GPU memory ECC codes Xid 63/64/94/95 (row-remap failure, contained/uncontained ECC) ≈ 2%. Table V maps each Xid code to its likely cause (hardware vs. software vs. driver). LIMITS: it does NOT expose per-event
- **Access:** Public, open access. No standalone data repository — figures/tables are in the paper. arXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.14158 (HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2408.14158v1). Published in SC'24 (IEEE/ACM), DOI 10.1109/SC41406.2024.00089.
- **Used by:** Primary source: An et al., "Fire-Flyer AI-HPC: A Cost-Effective Software-Hardware Co-Design for Deep Learning," SC'24. Cited as a reference point on AI-cluster GPU failures by e.g. Cui et al. "Characterizing GPU Resilience and Impact on AI/HPC Systems" (arXiv:2503.11901) and industry write-ups (Grad
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (a dedicated LLM/DL training cluster). Directly relevant to the "do AI workloads fail more, and why" question because it gives a real production AI-cluster fault breakdown dominated by interconnect faults (NVLink 42.6%) and user/software memory faults (Xid43 33.5%), with ECC/memory a small t
- **Caveats:** Not a real "dataset": no released raw logs, no per-event/per-node records, no timestamps or exit codes — only aggregate percentages, one categorization table, and a trend figure. Percentages are point-in-time aggregates over ~1 year; denominators (total error count) and exact node/GPU counts underlying each figure are not fully specified. The 42.57%/33.48%/~2% figures come from the paper text/figu
- **Confidence:** high (dataset/characterization confirmed

### From Detection to Recovery: Operational Analysis on LLM Pre-training with 504 GPUs
*aka:* Lablup 504-GPU operational failure analysis; Lablup Technical Report (arXiv 2605.09370)
*Org:* Lablup (lead); joint operating collaboration with SKT, Upstage, NVIDIA Korea, VAST Data  
*System:* 63-node NVIDIA DGX B200 production LLM pre-training cluster (Solar Open / Upstage training run)

### Meta ML Research Cluster Reliability Data ("Revisiting Reliability in Large-Scale Machine Learning Research Clusters", arXiv:2410.21680)

Root cause is attributed by a temporal heuristic, not ground-truth labels: a failure is attributed to a detected cause if that cause was observed within ~10 min

- **Used by:** Authored by Meta (Kokolis, Kuchnik, Hoffman, A. Kumar, Malani, Ma, DeVito, Sengupta, Saladi, Carole-Jean Wu). - **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (dedicated large-scale ML/GPU training clusters), but methodologically bridges to HPC reliability studies (MTTF modeling, failure taxonomy) so it is directly comparable to HPC system logs. - **Caveats:** Key gap: the downloadable trace labels outcomes only as Pass/Killed/Failed with NO exit codes, error strings, or fault categories — do not assume the paper's infrastructure/AI-engine/user failure breakdown is reconstructable from the public files. ### Philly Trace (Microsoft Philly GPU cluster trace)
*aka:* Philly Traces; MSR-Fiddle Philly trace; Project Philly DNN scheduler trace
*Org:* Microsoft Research (Project Fiddle / Project Philly)  
*System:* Microsoft Philly — internal multi-tenant GPU cluster for DNN training  
*Period:* ~2 months of scheduler logs (jobs submitted Aug 7 – Dec 22, 2017)  
*Scale:* 117,325 jobs across multiple VCs/users; cluster of many machines with 8 GPUs each (per-minute GPU/CPU/mem utilization logged); ~6.6 GB uncompressed (0.98 GB com
- **Contents:** Per-job scheduler records: job status, submit time, per-attempt scheduling timestamps and retries, server IPs, number and placement of GPUs allocated, anonymized (hashed) user and virtual-cluster IDs; plus per-minute per-machine GPU utilization (8 GPUs/machine), CPU utilization, memory (total/free), and a machine list with GPU counts and memory specs.
- **Failure info:** PARTIAL / coarse. The released trace labels each job with one of three terminal outcomes — Pass, Killed, or Failed — and gives per-attempt scheduling history (so retries and re-scheduling of failed attempts are visible). That is the extent of the failure labeling in the public data: NO exit codes, NO error messages/stack traces, and NO root-cause category are present as fields. The widely cited failure taxonomy — 4,960 failed jobs classified into 20 failure categories across 4 dimensions (e.g., GPU/CPU out-of-memory, syntax/semantic errors, runtime, etc.), with root causes manually analyzed for ~400 — was produced BY the accompanying papers (Jeon et al. ATC'19, and in most detail the ICSE'20
- **Access:** Public. GitHub repo https://github.com/msr-fiddle/philly-traces (CC-BY-4.0); the trace tarball is downloaded via the repo's script/link. Cite the ATC'19 paper when using it.
- **Used by:** Jeon, Venkataraman, Phanishayee, Qian, Xiao, Yang, "Analysis of Large-Scale Multi-Tenant GPU Clusters for DNN Training Workloads," USENIX ATC 2019 (original release). Zhang et al., "An Empirical Study on Program Failures of Deep Learning Jobs," ICSE 2020 (the 4,960-failures / 20-category taxonomy). 
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only. Purely DNN/DL training workloads on a GPU cluster with a DL-aware scheduler; no traditional HPC/MPI batch jobs. Directly usable for the AI side of "do AI workloads fail more than HPC" — it supplies the AI failure rate evidence (>1/3 of GPU time reportedly wasted on failed/killed jobs; high 
- **Caveats:** Coverage/label gaps: (1) Public trace has only 3 coarse status labels; the celebrated 20-category root-cause taxonomy and the "4,960 failures / >1/3 GPU-time wasted" figures come from the ATC'19 and ICSE'20 papers using internal logs NOT in the release, so those cause labels are not reproducible from the downloadable data. (2) The seed note attributes "4,960 failures / 20 categories" to Jeon ATC'1

### Revisiting Reliability in Large-Scale Machine Learning Research Clusters (Meta RSC-1 / RSC-2)
*aka:* Meta Research SuperCluster (RSC) reliability study; "Revisiting Reliability" (HPCA 2025); Kokolis et al. 2024/2025
*Org:* Meta (Meta AI / FAIR + infrastructure teams)  
*System:* Two Meta multi-tenant ML research clusters, RSC-1 and RSC-2 (Meta Research SuperCluster)  
*Period:* 11 months of production data (v1 Oct 2024; v2 Feb 2025; published at IEEE HPCA 2025)  
*Scale:* ~4 million jobs; 150+ million A100 GPU-hours; DGX-style servers with NVIDIA A100 80GB GPUs (8 GPUs/node). RSC-1 is the larger of the two clusters; jobs range fr
- **Contents:** Job-level and infrastructure telemetry aggregated into published statistics: job scheduling states, job sizes/durations, failure and interruption rates, health-check signals, and derived reliability metrics. Introduces reliability metrics including Mean Time to Failure (MTTF) fitted as a function of GPU scale, and an "Effective Training Time Ratio (ETTR)" to quantify wasted vs productive training time. Also models efficacy of software mitigations (checkpointing, health checks, hot spares) at scale. Data is presented only as charts/tables in the paper.
- **Failure info:** This is the strongest asset of the work but it is a PUBLISHED ANALYSIS, not a labeled trace you can download. The paper DOES define an explicit failure taxonomy with three failure domains — User Program (OOM, user bugs), System Software (GPU driver/firmware, OS, PyTorch, filesystem mounts, NCCL timeouts, system services), and Hardware Infrastructure (GPU unavailable, GPU memory/ECC errors, NVLink errors, PCIe errors, InfiniBand/Ethernet link errors, main-memory errors). It maps to Slurm job-exit states (FAILED with non-zero exit code, NODE_FAIL, OUT_OF_MEMORY, TIMEOUT, PREEMPTED, REQUEUED, CANCELLED, COMPLETED), so exit-code/interruption semantics are discussed. Root-cause ATTRIBUTION is pro
- **Access:** Restricted / not released. The paper is open access (arXiv 2410.21680: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.21680; HPCA 2025; Meta page https://ai.meta.com/research/publications/revisiting-reliability-in-large-scale-machine-learning-research-clusters/) but the underlying raw traces/logs are proprietary Meta data and are NOT publicly available. Only aggregated statistics, taxonomy, and models in the paper ar
- **Used by:** Primary source: Kokolis, Kuchnik, Hoffman, Kumar, Malani, Ma, DeVito, Sengupta, Saladi, Wu, "Revisiting Reliability in Large-Scale Machine Learning Research Clusters," IEEE HPCA 2025. Widely cited as a reference/benchmark in subsequent LLM-training reliability and fault-tolerance work (e.g., fault-t
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (large-scale GPU ML training on A100 research superclusters). Highly relevant to the "do AI workloads fail more than HPC and why" question: it quantifies AI-training MTTF vs GPU scale, shows large jobs are disproportionately failure-prone while small jobs dominate by count, and attributes fa
- **Caveats:** Not a dataset in the downloadable sense — no raw traces, no per-job records, no exit-code table released; all numbers must be read off the paper's charts. Root-cause labels are heuristic and self-described as "noisy," so attribution percentages carry uncertainty. Coverage is A100 only (no H100 despite the seed note; H100 is not analyzed here). Single vendor/site (Meta RSC), so representativeness o
- **Confidence:** high — dataset/paper confirmed to exist 

### SenseTime Helios GPU Trace (HeliosData)
*aka:* HeliosData; Helios Traces from SenseTime; Helios (Earth / Saturn / Uranus / Venus clusters)
*Org:* SenseTime (Helios datacenter); released by S-Lab (SCALE Lab), Nanyang Technological University (NTU) System Group  
*System:* SenseTime Helios private DL datacenter — 4 of 8 clusters publicly released: Earth, Saturn, Uranus, Venus  
*Period:* 6 months, April 2020 – September 2020  
*Scale:* 4 clusters, ~802 nodes / 6,416 GPUs (Helios total: 8 clusters, >12,000 GPUs). Trace: 3,362,981 total jobs, of which 1,580,464 are GPU jobs. Release size ~343MB 
- **Contents:** Per-cluster cluster_log.csv job records: job_id, user, vc (virtual cluster), gpu_num, cpu_num, node_num, state, submit_time, start_time, end_time, duration (plus queue/wait time). Plus cluster_gpu_number.csv giving daily GPU allocation per virtual cluster. Job-level scheduling/telemetry only — no per-step or hardware time-series, no resource-utilization samples, no logs.
- **Failure info:** DOES label final job status — the `state` field carries one of COMPLETED, CANCELLED, FAILED, TIMEOUT, NODE_FAIL (TIMEOUT and NODE_FAIL are noted as very rare). So it gives an explicit FAILED (and NODE_FAIL) outcome label per job and lets you compute failure rates and cost of failed jobs. HOWEVER it does NOT expose exit codes, error/return codes, stack traces, logs, or any root-cause / failure-reason attribution — you know a job FAILED, not why (application bug vs OOM vs hardware vs preemption are indistinguishable). Cancellation intent (user-cancel vs system) is also not disaggregated. Failure characterization in the SC'21 paper is therefore statistical (e.g., short jobs and certain users/VC
- **Access:** Public, CC-BY-4.0. GitHub: https://github.com/S-Lab-System-Group/HeliosData ; paper DOI https://doi.org/10.1145/3458817.3476223 (arXiv:2109.01313).
- **Used by:** Primary: Hu, Sun, Yan, Wen, Zhang, "Characterization and Prediction of Deep Learning Workloads in Large-Scale GPU Datacenters," SC'21 (arXiv:2109.01313). Reused widely as a DL-scheduling benchmark, e.g. Chronus (S-Lab), Lucid, and referenced in surveys "Deep Learning Workload Scheduling in GPU Datac
- **AI vs HPC:** AI-only (deep-learning GPU cluster trace). Strong for the "do AI workloads fail more, and how costly" side: it is one of the largest DL-cluster traces and, unlike training-only traces, includes non-training / inference / debug job types, so you can compare failure rates across DL job classes. Its li
- **Caveats:** Node/GPU counts per released cluster (802 nodes / 6,416 GPUs for the 4 public clusters) come from the paper's characterization, not a schema field; Helios overall is 8 clusters / >12k GPUs. Key gaps: no exit codes, no failure root cause, no resource-utilization/telemetry time series, and only a 6-month 2020 window — pre-LLM-era, so job-size/failure mix may not reflect current large-model training.


## GPU hardware errors & reliability  (11)

### A Large-Scale Study of Soft-Errors on GPUs in the Field (Nie et al., HPCA 2016)
*aka:* Titan GPU soft-error field study; Nie 2016 HPCA. NOTE: this is Titan-only (K20X); the "Blue Waters" pairing in the seed is a conflation — Blue Waters K20X soft-error studies are a separate line (Marti
*Org:* ORNL (Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility / UT-Battelle); analysis by College of William & Mary, ORNL, Intel Labs, Northeastern  
*System:* Titan supercomputer (Cray XK7), OLCF/ORNL  
*Period:* Field logs from Titan in production; the closely-related companion study (Nie et al., MASCOTS 2017) uses Feb 2015–Jun 2015. The HPCA'16 study draws on Titan RAS  
*Scale:* 18,688 NVIDIA Tesla K20X GPUs (6 GB GDDR5 each), one GPU + one 16-core AMD Opteron 6274 per node; 200 cabinets arranged in a 25x8 grid; the companion MASCOTS'17
- **Contents:** Per-GPU-node soft-error counts collected via nvidia-smi (SBE = single-bit correctable ECC errors, the primary focus; DBE = double-bit uncorrectable errors also present; memory protected by SECDED ECC). SBE counts are snapshotted before/after each executed application (aprun/"batch job"), so counts are at batch-job granularity, not per-individual-error timestamps. Also includes out-of-band per-node telemetry sampled every minute: GPU temperature and power consumption; plus per-aprun resource utilization (GPU core-hours, max and total memory consumption). Node location (cabinet/cage/slot) is recorded, enabling spatial analysis.
- **Failure info:** Labels HARDWARE SOFT-ERROR EVENTS by ECC type, not job/task failures. Each record is an error-TYPE-labeled count: SBE (single-bit, correctable — do not crash jobs) vs DBE (double-bit, uncorrectable). Root cause is implicit and singular — memory/logic bit-flips (transient soft errors) in ECC-protected components (register files, cache, DRAM); it does NOT diagnose per-event root cause beyond the ECC type, and by design (nvidia-smi snapshot-diff) it CANNOT associate an individual SBE with a timestamp or a specific job outcome. It does NOT record job exit codes, XID codes per event (NVIDIA XID causes are only referenced, not enumerated in the data), scheduler exit status, or task success/failure
- **Access:** Restricted / on-request. The raw Titan soft-error logs are ORNL-internal and were not released as an open dataset with the paper. Paper (the deliverable of record): IEEE HPCA 2016, DOI 10.1109/HPCA.2016.7446091; also at https://www.researchgate.net/publication/299641571 and via IEEE Xplore. A related PUBLIC dataset exists but is different: "GPU Lifetimes on Titan Supercomputer" (Ostrouchov et al.,
- **Used by:** Nie et al., "Machine Learning Models for GPU Error Prediction in a Large Scale HPC System" (DSN 2018, par.nsf.gov/10065578) — the prediction companion; Nie et al., "Characterizing Temperature, Power, and Soft-Error Behaviors in Data Center Systems" (MASCOTS 2017, par.nsf.gov/10065577) — the temp/pow
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. Workloads are 2015-era scientific/simulation applications on Titan (pre-deep-learning). It contains NO AI/ML training jobs. Its value for the "do AI workloads fail more than HPC, and why" question is as an HPC-side BASELINE: authoritative field soft-error (SBE/DBE) rates, spatial/temporal 
- **Caveats:** (1) The seed's "Titan/Blue Waters" framing is inaccurate — Nie 2016 is Titan K20X only. (2) The most important limitation for failure analysis: SBE data is collected as nvidia-smi count snapshots diffed around each batch job, so individual errors have NO precise timestamp and CANNOT be tied to a specific job's success/failure; the paper conservatively assumes SBEs are attributed across all apruns 
- **Confidence:** high (paper and its data-collection meth

*aka:* Blue Waters Kepler GPU failure data; Blue Waters XK7 GPU accelerator failure/reliability data
*Org:* NCSA / University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign (National Petascale Computing Facility)  
*System:* NCSA Blue Waters (Cray XK7 hybrid; 4,228 XK compute nodes each with one NVIDIA Tesla K20X "GK110" Kepler GPU, 6 GB GDDR5 w/ SECDED ECC)  
*Period:* ~2013-2017 production era (K20X GPUs); specific windows: 261 days in Di Martino et al. 2014; full-lifetime reliability trends in Bode et al. 2024

- **Contents:** Blue Waters failure and reliability characterization covering the GPU accelerator among other components (CPU, DIMM/DRAM, network, disk, PSU, blower, filesystem): single-node failure root-cause categories, component-level statistical failure rates over time, and system-level failover/outage analysis. For the K20X specifically: GPU-related node failures and hardware failure rates. Detailed per-GPU ECC single-bit/double-bit (SBE/DBE) memory-error logs of the kind published for Titan are NOT part of a public Blue Waters release; Blue Waters is used mainly as a cross-system comparison point.
- **Failure info:** This IS failure-labeled data, not raw telemetry — but at the system/component reliability level, not a raw GPU memory-error log. Di Martino et al. (DSN 2014) categorize root causes of single-node failures and classify GPU-accelerator error/resiliency events (including hardware vs. transient, error propagation, and failover outcomes) from manual failure reports plus automated event logs. Bode et al. (2024, CCPE) report per-component statistical failure counts/rates over the machine lifetime, explicitly including GPU hardware failures, and flag failure-rate increases (aging/wear-out). What is present: failure/fault events with root-cause classification and failure rates for the GPU component. 
- **Access:** Restricted / on-request effectively. No confirmed public dataset download. System specs page https://bluewaters.ncsa.illinois.edu/hardware-summary is hardware-only (no error data). Findings accessible via papers: Di Martino et al., DSN 2014, "Lessons Learned from the Analysis of System Failures at Petascale: The Case of Blue Waters," DOI 10.1109/DSN.2014.62; Bode et al., 2024, "Blue Waters system 
- **Used by:** Di Martino et al. DSN 2014 (primary Blue Waters failure analysis); Bode et al. 2024 CCPE (component reliability trends); cited as a K20X comparison point in cross-generation GPU reliability work, e.g., Zhu et al. 2025 "Understanding the Landscape of Ampere GPU Memory Errors" (arXiv:2508.03513, which
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. K20X/Kepler-era (2013-2017) traditional-HPC accelerator data; predates modern AI-training GPU clusters (no NVLink/HBM, no large-model training workloads). Useful for cross-generation GPU reliability comparison (K20X -> V100 -> A100) and as a Kepler baseline, but it does NOT directly answer
- **Caveats:** The seed framing "K20X GPU error records / memory-error logs" overstates what is publicly available. Confirmed: (1) Blue Waters existed with 4,228 K20X GPUs; (2) its failure/reliability data including the GPU component was analyzed and published (Di Martino DSN 2014; Bode 2024). NOT confirmed: any downloadable, standalone Blue Waters K20X GPU error-record dataset with raw ECC SBE/DBE counts or exi
- **Confidence:** medium

### Experimental Findings on the Sources of Detected Unrecoverable Errors in GPUs
*aka:* DUE neutron-beam GPU study; arXiv 2108.00554; dos Santos et al. NSREC/IEEE TNS
*Org:* Univ. Grenoble Alpes/INRIA + UFRGS (dos Santos, Carro) with Politecnico di Milano/Univ. of Padova (Rech); beam time at STFC ISIS ChipIR (Cazzaniga, Frost)  
*System:* NVIDIA Kepler (Tesla K20/K40 class) and Volta (Tesla V100/Titan V class) GPUs under accelerated neutron irradiation  
*Period:* Beam campaigns circa 2019-2021; paper posted arXiv Aug 2021, presented NSREC 2021  
*Scale:* Two GPU architectures (Kepler, Volta); results reported as aggregated error/cross-section statistics over accumulated neutron fluence, not a per-node or per-job
- **Contents:** Experimentally measured neutron-induced error data: DUE (Detected Unrecoverable Error) counts and cross-sections broken down by source class — illegal memory/address access, interface errors, and launch failures — with ECC on vs off. Also reports how ECC shifts the distribution (increases launch-failure events while cutting illegal-address DUEs). Underlying signals are GPU/driver-reported error events (Xid-class DUEs) captured during irradiation, not application telemetry time series.
- **Failure info:** This is the strongest part of the study and the key distinction: it explicitly LABELS and CATEGORIZES failure/fault types by root-cause class. Every DUE is attributed to a source: illegal address access, interface (bus/link) error, or kernel launch failure — i.e. it goes beyond raw telemetry to give a characterized failure taxonomy and quantifies each class's cross-section and ECC sensitivity. However, it does NOT provide per-event exit codes, job IDs, timestamps, or a row-level fault log; the "failure info" is aggregate categorized cross-sections and reduction percentages (ECC cuts illegal-address DUEs up to ~92% Kepler / ~98% Volta), a reliability characterization rather than a labeled ope
- **Access:** public (paper only): preprint https://arxiv.org/abs/2108.00554 (PDF https://arxiv.org/pdf/2108.00554); peer-reviewed version in IEEE Transactions on Nuclear Science. No confirmed public data-repository link for the raw beam measurements.
- **Used by:** Cited within the ISIS ChipIR / dos Santos-Rech GPU-reliability line of work — e.g. "Demystifying GPU Reliability: Comparing and Combining Beam Experiments, Fault Simulation, and Profiling" (IPDPS 2021); "Response of HPC hardware to neutron radiation at the dawn of exascale" (J. Supercomputing 2023);
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC/hardware-reliability oriented (both HPC and AI GPUs share these silicon-level DUE mechanisms). It characterizes the physical fault sources of GPU unrecoverable errors, so it is useful for explaining WHY GPU nodes fail (illegal-address / interface / launch DUEs, ECC effects) — but it cannot answe
- **Caveats:** Not a production log dataset — accelerated-beam experimental data, so absolute rates are fluence-scaled, not field FIT rates directly. No confirmed downloadable raw-data artifact; only aggregated results in the paper, so the record's data-level fields (format/scale) are limited by what the PDF publishes. Failure attribution is per-category cross-section, not per-event rows with exit codes/timestam
- **Confidence:** high that the paper and its categorized 

### GPU Lifetimes on Titan Supercomputer: Survival Analysis and Reliability
*aka:* TitanGPULife; Ostrouchov Titan GPU survival dataset
*Org:* ORNL / OLCF (Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility)  
*System:* ORNL Titan (Cray XK7), 18,688 NVIDIA Tesla K20X GPUs  
*Period:* ~2013–2019 (Titan's productive period; paper cites ~6 years / >100k GPU-years). Data published Sept 2, 2020.  
*Scale:* 18,688 GPUs (plus service-node GPUs); >100,000 collective GPU-years of operation over Titan's ~6-year productive life. Per-GPU records keyed by serial number an
- **Contents:** Four data artifacts plus analysis code: titan.gpu.history.txt (raw operational/GPU event history), titan.service.txt (service-node exclusion list), gc_full.csv (cleaned/processed event data), and gc_summary_loc.csv (aggregated per-GPU records). Summary fields include: SN (serial number), time, nlife, nloc, last, col, row, cage, slot, node, max_loc_events, time_max_loc, dbe, dbe_loc, otb, otb_loc, out, batch, days, years, dead, dead_otb, dead_dbe. Physical placement enables the paper's heat-dissipation/cooling-position reliability analysis.
- **Failure info:** Labels HARDWARE failure types explicitly. Two GPU failure modes are coded and used as survival-analysis events: DBE (Double Bit Error, uncorrectable ECC memory error) and OTB (Out-of-the-Bus / GPU falls off the PCIe bus / dropped node), plus a catch-all "out" for other terminations. Per-GPU death indicators are provided (dead, dead_otb, dead_dbe) along with time-to-event fields (time, days, years, nlife). Root cause is at the hardware-mode level (DBE vs OTB) and the paper attributes reliability to physical position/heat dissipation, but the dataset does NOT contain job/application exit codes, software error codes, or per-job root-cause logs — it is hardware lifetime/telemetry with fault-mode
- **Access:** Public. DOI: https://doi.org/10.13139/ORNLNCCS/1657202 (OSTI/DOE Data Explorer https://www.osti.gov/dataexplorer/biblio/dataset/1657202 ; Constellation/ORNL CCS mirror). Data + code on GitHub: https://github.com/olcf/TitanGPULife . Publisher ORNL/OLCF; license not explicitly stated on the landing page but distributed openly via DOE Data Explorer and public GitHub.
- **Used by:** Primary: Ostrouchov, Maxwell, Ashraf, Engelmann, Shankar, Rogers, "GPU Lifetimes on Titan Supercomputer: Survival Analysis and Reliability," SC '20 (IEEE/ACM), doi:10.1109/SC41405.2020.00045 (IEEE Xplore 9355319). The dataset is the companion to this paper and is cited in subsequent GPU/accelerator 
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only (hardware reliability). It is a GPU-hardware-failure lifetime dataset from an HPC leadership machine; it contains no AI/ML training-job telemetry or job-failure semantics. For the question "do AI workloads fail more than HPC and why," it is useful only as an HPC-side hardware baseline: it q
- **Caveats:** Hardware-fault-mode labels only (DBE, OTB, other) — no job exit codes, no software/application error causes, no per-job attribution. "Root cause" is inferred at the physical/thermal level via GPU location fields, not recorded as a causal field. K20X-specific (older Kepler GPUs), single system (Titan), so generalization to modern AI GPUs (A100/H100) and NVLink/HBM failure modes is limited. License 
- **Confidence:** high — dataset existence, DOI, contents,

### Nie et al. Titan GPU error trace (DSN 2018) — related public release: "GPU Lifetimes on Titan" (TitanGPULife)
*aka:* ORNL Titan GPU error logs; "Titan GPU SXM error logs" (SXM is a misnomer — see caveats); TitanGPULife; Ostrouchov/Shankar GPU lifetime dataset
*Org:* ORNL / Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility (OLCF); analysis co-authored with Northeastern (Tiwari) and College of William & Mary (Smirni)  
*System:* Titan supercomputer (Cray XK7), NVIDIA Tesla K20X GPUs (one K20X per XK7 node)

*Scale:* 18,688 K20X GPUs physically installed; cumulative >30,000 GPU units over the machine's life once field replacements are counted (public lifetime dataset tracks 
- **Contents:** Per-event GPU hardware error records plus co-located telemetry and topology features. Error events: single-bit ECC errors (SBE, corrected), double-bit ECC errors (DBE, uncorrectable memory/register-file errors), and off-the-bus (OTB, GPU lost from the host/PCIe). Features used for prediction: GPU/node temperature, power consumption, application (aprun/job) state, and full physical location (node, board, cabinet, row/column position in the machine room). The public TitanGPULife CSVs (gc_full.csv, titan.gpu.history.txt) give ~19 variables per GPU: serial number, install/removal times, operating duration, physical location, and failure-type classification, structured as survival/time-to-event d
- **Failure info:** This is a genuinely failure-LABELED dataset, not raw telemetry. Every recorded event carries an explicit hardware fault-class label: SBE, DBE, or OTB. That label is effectively a coarse root-cause category — DBE = uncorrectable GPU memory/register-file ECC fault (~86% memory, remainder register file), OTB = loss of host-to-GPU connectivity (bus/link/firmware). The public lifetime release adds survival labels (failure vs. right-censored) and time-to-failure per GPU by serial number. ERRCODE gives fine-grained fault type (e.g. "DetectedClockCardErrors"), spanning 82 ERRCODE types across 6 components among FATAL events; COMPONENT/SUBCOMPONENT/LOCATION localize the fault. - **Caveats:** (1) Date discrepancy: abstract says "273-day" but the dataset table and filename (0901_0908) give 237 days, Jan–Aug 2009 — use 237 days. *aka:* Mira 5-year RAS dataset; LogAider fatal-events dataset; ALCF Mira RAS log
*Org:* Argonne National Laboratory (ALCF)

- **Contents:** System-generated RAS event stream (not job/workload telemetry). The CTLACTION field records the system's automated response (the closest thing to an impact/handling label). *Org:* NCSA / University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign (research by Di Martino, Jha, Kramer, Kalbarczyk, Iyer; DEPEND/CSL group)

*Scale:* Blue Waters full system: ~27,648 nodes (22,640 Cray XE6 CPU nodes + 4,228 XK7 CPU+GPU nodes), ~13.3 PF peak; studies span millions of node-hours and hundreds of
- **Contents:** Joint analysis of several sources: (1) workload logs (jobs and the applications launched inside them, XE CPU vs XK CPU+GPU node-hours); (2) system error/failure logs (RAS, console, hardware sensors); (3) manual failure reports authored by NCSA operations staff. LogDiver filters/extracts/classifies errors, extracts signals from categorized errors, consolidates per-application data, decodes application and job exit status, and correlates application failures with underlying system errors, yielding the well-known log-log relation between application node-hours and failure probability.

The work explicitly distinguishes system-caused from user-caused application failures (reporting that ~1.53% of applications failed due to system-related issues) and finds XK (GPU) applications more failure-prone than XE (CPU-only). - **Access:** Restricted / not publicly released. - **AI vs HPC:** HPC/grid-only. This is a mid-2000s grid resource-availability trace with NO AI/ML workload content and NO job-level or GPU information. *System:* Five production supercomputers: Blue Gene/L (LLNL), and Thunderbird, RedStorm, Spirit, Liberty (Sandia)

- **Used by:** Oliner & Stearley, "What Supercomputers Say: A Study of Five System Logs," DSN 2007 (origin paper); Oliner, Aiken & Stearley, "Alert Detection in System Logs," ICDM 2008; Stearley & Oliner, "Bad Words: Finding Faults in Spirit's Syslogs," Resilience/CCGrid 2008; He et al., "Loghub: A Large Collectio
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. These are classic (2004-2006) capability-supercomputer RAS/syslogs running scientific MPI workloads; there are NO AI/ML training jobs represented. - **Caveats:** Alert labels are curated semi-automatically and contain label noise; categories are coarse alert types, not validated faults. ### LANL failure data (22 HPC systems, 1996-2005)
*aka:* LANL node outage data; LA-UR-05-7318; Schroeder-Gibson LANL data; CFDR "The LANL data"; "los-alamos-national-lab-data"
*Org:* Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL); released/hosted via USENIX Computer Failure Data Repository (CFDR), curated by Bianca Schroeder & Garth Gibson (CMU PDL)  
*System:* 22 production HPC systems at LANL (labeled A-H by hardware type), mix of NUMA and 2-/4-way SMP clusters; node counts 1-1024 per system  
*Period:* June 1996 - November 2005 (~9 years of production time)  
*Scale:* 4,750 nodes / 24,101 processors across the 22 systems; 23,741 failure (node-outage) records total
- **Contents:** One record per node outage that required system-administrator intervention. Fields per record (confirmed from Table 2 of CMU-PDL-05-112): Starttime (failure began), Endtime (repaired/back in job mix), System ID, Node number, Type of Node/workload (compute / graphics / fe front-end), and Root cause with two levels: High-level category + a Detailed sub-cause. A companion system table gives per-system hardware architecture (NUMA/SMP), node/proc counts, procs-per-node, commission/production/decommission dates, memory (GB), and NIC count. Sourced from LANL's site-wide "remedy" trouble-ticket database (a site policy since June 1996 required admins to log every intervention).
- **Failure info:** This is a true labeled-failure dataset, not raw telemetry. Every record IS a failure event (an admin-attended node outage), so there is no ambiguous "is this a failure" question. Each carries a manually assigned root cause at two granularities: a HIGH-LEVEL category (one of six: Hardware, Software, Network, Environment, Human, plus Unknown/Undetermined) AND a DETAILED sub-cause (e.g. "Cooling Fan" under Hardware, "OS Software" under Software). Aggregate breakdown: ~53% Hardware, ~22% Software, remainder Network/Environment/Human/Unknown (Unknown share is significant). What it does NOT contain: no process/job exit codes, no application-level or job-level failure records, no automated error-lo
- **Access:** Public, free. Original LANL FTP (ftp://hpc-ftp.lanl.gov/data/failure/LA-UR-05-7318-failure-data-1996-2005.tar.gz) is defunct; the canonical live mirror is the USENIX CFDR: https://www.usenix.org/cfdr-data and the dataset page https://www.usenix.org/lanl-data . Cleared for public release as LA-UR-05-7318. (Note: USENIX pages currently 403 to automated fetchers but resolve in a normal browser.)
- **Used by:** Schroeder & Gibson, "A Large-Scale Study of Failures in HPC Systems," DSN 2006 and IEEE Trans. Dependable & Secure Computing 7(4):337-350, 2010 (primary/originating study; pre-print CMU-PDL-05-112). Widely reused as the flagship public HPC failure set, e.g. Cappello et al. fault-tolerance-at-exascal
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. Predates GPU/accelerator and AI/ML training workloads entirely (1996-2005; workloads are large-scale scientific simulation, e.g. nuclear-stockpile codes with periodic checkpointing). It cannot directly show whether AI workloads fail more, but it is the canonical HPC BASELINE for the compar
- **Caveats:** Root causes are human-entered admin labels, so misdiagnosis and a non-trivial "Unknown/Undetermined" fraction are real label noise. Records only capture failures an administrator attended (node-outage granularity) — no fine-grained error logs, no job-level or exit-code data, no per-application attribution. ### LANL HPC Failure Data (Schroeder & Gibson / CFDR LA-UR node-outage logs)
*aka:* Los Alamos 9-year failure data; LANL node-outage logs; "LANL data" in the Computer Failure Data Repository (CFDR); the DSN 2006 Schroeder-Gibson failure dataset
*Org:* Los Alamos National Laboratory (LANL); released/hosted via CMU Parallel Data Lab + USENIX Computer Failure Data Repository (CFDR), curated by Bianca Schroeder &  
*System:* 22 HPC systems in production at LANL (numbered Systems 1-24, mostly large clusters of SMP and NUMA nodes; a few single large SMPs). Cross-machine failure/RAS lo  
*Period:* ~9 years, 1996 through November 2005  
*Scale:* 4,750 nodes and 24,101 processors across the 22 systems; ~23,000 failure records (commonly cited as 23,741). One record per node outage requiring sysadmin atten
- **Contents:** One record per failure that caused a node outage and required a system administrator's attention. Each record holds: outage start time and end time (so per-outage downtime/repair time is derivable), the system ID and node affected, and a categorized root cause. Node/system hardware descriptors (processor type/count, node count, install/decommission dates, hardware generation) are provided per system so failures can be normalized by node count and age. Also supports time-between-failure / node-outage inter-arrival analysis. It is anonymized (systems and vendors are numbered, not named).
- **Failure info:** This is the dataset's core strength: it DOES explicitly label root cause, not just telemetry. Every one of the ~23,000 records carries an administrator-assigned root cause in a curated taxonomy of five high-level categories — Hardware, Software, Network, Environment (power/AC/etc.), and Human error — plus finer sub-categories (e.g. specific failing hardware component, software module). A residual "Undetermined/Unknown" bucket exists where the cause was never diagnosed. Distribution: Hardware is largest (>50% of failures), Software second (~20%). Start/end timestamps give outage duration. IMPORTANT LIMITS: it does NOT expose exit codes, job IDs, or job/application-level failure info — applica
- **Access:** Public. Landing/description page https://www.usenix.org/cfdr-data and the LANL-specific page https://www.usenix.org/lanl-data (historically cfdr.usenix.org); mirrored via CMU PDL Failure Data project https://www.pdl.cmu.edu/FailureData/index.shtml. Primary paper: Schroeder & Gibson, "A Large-Scale Study of Failures in High-Performance Computing Systems," DSN 2006, DOI 10.1109/DSN.2006.5 (open PDF 
- **Used by:** Schroeder & Gibson, "A Large-Scale Study of Failures in HPC Systems," DSN 2006 (the origin analysis); Schroeder & Gibson, "Understanding Failures in Petascale Computers," J. Physics Conf. Series / SciDAC 2007; the seed dataset that launched the USENIX/CMU CFDR. Widely reused as the canonical HPC fai
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. Pre-2006 capability/cluster machines with no GPUs and no AI/DL workloads, so it cannot directly speak to AI-workload failures. Its value for the "do AI workloads fail more than HPC, and why" question is as the authoritative HPC baseline for node-outage rates, per-node MTBF, failure-rate sc
- **Caveats:** (1) No job-level data — no exit codes, job IDs, runtimes, or user/application info; application failures are deliberately excluded, so it does not measure job failure rate. (2) Root cause is human-assigned and includes an "Undetermined" bucket, so cause labels carry noise and coverage gaps, especially for the earliest years and hard-to-diagnose events. (3) Only failures serious enough to need sysa
- **Confidence:** high — existence, scale (22 systems / 4,

### Loghub HPC RAS log collection (BGL, Thunderbird, Liberty, Spirit)
*aka:* Loghub; logpai/loghub; the Sandia/LLNL RAS supercomputer logs (originally Oliner & Stearley "What Supercomputers Say")
*Org:* LLNL (BlueGene/L) and Sandia National Laboratories (Thunderbird, Spirit, Liberty); redistributed by LogPAI (CUHK) via Zenodo  
*System:* BlueGene/L (LLNL); Thunderbird, Spirit, Liberty clusters (Sandia National Laboratories)  
*Period:* Logs collected ~2004-2006 era of the machines; individual spans: BGL 214.7 days, Thunderbird 244 days. Loghub collection paper published 2020 (arXiv:2008.06448)  
*Scale:* BGL: 4,747,963 lines / 708.76 MiB over 214.7 days. Thunderbird: 211,212,192 lines / 29.60 GiB over 244 days. Spirit: ~272,298,969 lines. Liberty: ~265,569,231 l
- **Contents:** Free-text RAS/system log messages (kernel, hardware, RAS, daemon, application events). Each raw line carries: an alert category tag (first column), timestamp/epoch, node/component identifiers, and the message text. BGL and Thunderbird are the most widely used; Loghub also ships 2k-line pre-parsed/structured samples with extracted event templates for benchmarking log parsers.
- **Failure info:** This is a LABELED dataset — its defining feature. Each log line has an alert-category tag in the first field: "-" means non-alert (treated as normal), and any non-dash string is an alert/failure label. In BGL those tags are human-curated alert category CODES (e.g., KERNDTLB, APPSEV, KERNMNTF, KERNTERM and similar) that give a COARSE fault/failure CATEGORY (kernel, memory/DTLB, app-severe, network, etc.). Thunderbird/Spirit/Liberty tags were generated/curated by Sandia administrators to flag alert events. So it DOES label failures and provides a coarse root-cause category via the alert code taxonomy. Caveats on precision: it does NOT expose process exit codes, job IDs, return codes, or a stru
- **Access:** Public / freely available for research with attribution. Repo: https://github.com/logpai/loghub . Downloads on Zenodo record 8196385 — BGL: https://zenodo.org/records/8196385/files/BGL.zip ; Thunderbird: https://zenodo.org/records/8196385/files/Thunderbird.tar.gz (Spirit/Liberty via same Zenodo collection / original Usenix CFDR repository). Loghub paper: arXiv:2008.06448.
- **Used by:** He et al., "Loghub: A Large Collection of System Log Datasets for AI-driven Log Analytics" (ISSRE 2023 / arXiv:2008.06448). Foundational originally from Oliner & Stearley, "What Supercomputers Say: A Study of Five System Logs" (DSN 2007). Heavily used by DeepLog, LogAnomaly, LogBERT, NeuralLog, LogL
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. This is classic supercomputer RAS/system-log data — no AI/ML training-job telemetry, no GPU/accelerator or distributed-training failures. It CANNOT directly answer "do AI workloads fail more than HPC and why": there is no AI-workload arm to compare against. Its value is as the canonical la
- **Caveats:** Labels are admin-assigned alert-category tags, not verified ground-truth failure records; known label noise and some duplicate/near-duplicate lines. Most published work collapses the tags to binary alert/non-alert, discarding the finer fault-category signal. No exit codes, job/return codes, or structured repair/root-cause metadata — "cause" is inferable only from the alert code + free-text message
- **Confidence:** high — dataset existence, systems, line

- **Contents:** Free-text RAS / console / syslog event lines. Each line carries a leading alert-label token, then structured fields: a numeric timestamp (epoch), a date code, the node/component name, a second (parsed) timestamp, date, node repeated, and message type/severity (e.g. RAS KERNEL INFO / FATAL), followed by the raw log message content. Content covers kernel, hardware (memory/ECC, cache, torus network, cards), APP, and machine-check events. This gives a binary alert/non-alert ground truth (BGL: ~348,460 alert lines of 4.75M ≈ 7.3% labeled anomalous) plus a coarse alert-category, and message-type fields expose severity (INFO/WARNING/ERROR/FATAL) and subsystem (KERNEL/APP/HARDWARE). What it does NOT provide: no process/job exit codes, no per-job pass/fail outcome, and no verified root-cause attribution. The alert 
- **Access:** Public. - **Used by:** Origin: A. Oliner & J. Stearley, "What Supercomputers Say: A Study of Five System Logs," DSN 2007. Redistribution: S. He et al., "Loghub: A Large Collection of System Log Datasets for AI-driven Log Analytics," arXiv:2008.06448 / ISSRE 2023. Heavily used as a benchmark by log anomaly-detection / pars
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only in origin — these are classic capability/capacity supercomputer RAS logs (BG/L, commodity Linux clusters), pre-dating GPU/LLM training workloads, so they contain no AI-training jobs. - **Caveats:** Alert labels are noisy and semi-automatically derived (Nodeinfo/filtering + manual curation) — false positives/negatives are documented by the authors; treat the ground truth as approximate. Labels are binary alert vs non-alert plus a coarse category, NOT failure root cause and NOT job outcomes/exit codes. *aka:* LANL failure data (USRC mirror); CFDR "LANL data"; Schroeder-Gibson HPC failure dataset; LA-UR-05-7318

- **Contents:** One record per node outage/interrupt that required system-administrator attention. - **Caveats:** (1) Machines are anonymized (system numbers, not names); vendor/model recoverable only via the specs spreadsheet at coarse granularity. ### F-DATA: A Fugaku Workload Dataset for Job-centric Predictive Modelling in HPC Systems
*aka:* Fugaku job dataset; F-DATA; "Raikon" likely refers to RIKEN R-CCS (dataset's origin institution)
*Org:* RIKEN R-CCS (data source) in collaboration with University of Bologna (Antici et al.). Data collected via Fugaku's Fujitsu job scheduler + power/performance mon  
*System:* Supercomputer Fugaku (Fujitsu A64FX Arm SVE), RIKEN Center for Computational Science (R-CCS), Kobe, Japan  
*Period:* March 2021 – April 2024 (~3 years, from when Fugaku left pre-production into general public availability). Distributed as 38 monthly chunks.  
*Scale:* ~24 million jobs. Fugaku = 158,976 A64FX nodes (world #1 on TOP500 2020-2022). Per-job features include nodes requested/allocated and cores.
- **Contents:** Per-job records with: job identity/metadata (hashed user ID, NLP-encoded job name, queue/usage class), submit/start/end timestamps and durations, requested vs allocated node/core counts, memory; power consumption (minpcon/avgpcon/maxpcon), energy in Watt-Hours (econ); raw hardware performance counters (perf1–perf6: execution cycles, arithmetic ops, memory transactions); and derived performance metrics — flops, memory bandwidth (mbwidth), operational intensity (opint), and a compute-bound vs memory-bound label (pclass). Job name is encoded with an NLP model to retain useful signal without leaking private info. Format: Parquet.
- **Failure info:** YES — this dataset explicitly labels job failure, which is its headline selling point. Two fields: (1) exit code `ec` — an integer in [0-255] straight from the scheduler indicating whether execution succeeded; (2) a derived `exit state` label with two values, "completed" or "failed", defined by the rule exit code 0 = completed, any non-zero = failed. IMPORTANT CAVEAT on failure semantics: there is NO root-cause / fault-category / error-message information — you get only the exit-status integer and the binary pass/fail derived from it. There is no distinction between a hardware fault, a job timeout/kill, a user application error, or an intentional non-zero return. The authors explicitly warn 
- **Access:** Public / open. Zenodo DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.11467483 (https://zenodo.org/records/11467483). Paper (open access): Nature Scientific Data, https://doi.org/10.1038/s41597-025-05633-1 (PMC12311113). Code/docs: https://github.com/francescoantici/F-DATA. License: CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 (attribution, non-commercial, no-derivatives).
- **Used by:** Primary/reference paper: Antici, Yamamoto, Domke, Bartolini, "F-DATA: A Fugaku Workload Dataset for Job-centric Predictive Modelling in HPC Systems," Nature Scientific Data 12, 2025 (article s41597-025-05633-1). Positioned by authors as a successor/complement to earlier HPC job traces (e.g., MOGon I
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only (general scientific batch workload on Fugaku; not an AI/ML training-cluster trace). Directly usable for the "why do jobs fail" question on the HPC side: it enables failure/exit-status prediction from power, performance, and resource features, and supports studying which job characteristics 
- **Caveats:** (1) Failure label is coarse: binary completed/failed derived solely from exit code; no fault taxonomy, no error strings, no root cause, no hardware-vs-software attribution. (2) Label noise: authors acknowledge "failed" jobs may include false positives (apps intentionally returning non-zero on success), and timeouts/preemptions are not separated from genuine crashes. (3) Job names and user IDs are

- **Contents:** Two linked data types per system: (1) scheduler accounting records — job ID, user/owner/group, account, queue, submit/eligible/start/end times, requested vs used resources (ncpus, mem, pmem, vmem, walltime, cput), exec_host node/core allocation, job name (anonymized for Anvil), and a job-status/record marker; (2) node-level performance telemetry from TACC Stats (Conte/Stampede) sampled ~every 10 min, plus six key performance metrics in v3 — covering CPU, GPU, memory, network, Lustre/I-O, and virtual-memory utilization for compute nodes and the interconnect. v2 also ships Conte downtime/outage logs. - **Failure info:** Failure signal comes from scheduler ACCOUNTING OUTCOMES, not human-labeled root cause. So the dataset DOES expose per-job exit codes / exit status and pass-vs-fail outcomes, and prior DCSL work (Mitra et al., "Cluster workload analytics revisited") classifies failures by exit code. It does NOT provide an authoritative root-cause label per job: the ~3,873 user support tickets that carry human-diagnosed causes were explicitly withheld for pr
- **Access:** Public / open. Browse and download per-dataset via web, or bulk via Globus, at https://www.datadepot.rcac.purdue.edu/sbagchi/fresco/ (also https://www.rcac.purdue.edu/fresco/). Code/seed repo: https://github.com/purdue-dcsl/fresco. Paper DOI: 10.1145/3708035.3736090
- **Used by:** McKerracher, Mukherjee, Kalyanam, Bagchi, "Fresco: A Public Multi-Institutional Dataset for Understanding HPC System Behavior and Dependability," PEARC 2025 (DOI 10.1145/3708035.3736090); Amvrosiadis/Mitra/Bagchi et al. FRESCO SC15 BoF ("An Open Failure Data Repository for Dependability Research"); 
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only in intent — three traditional academic batch-scheduler supercomputers; jobs are not tagged by workload type, so there is NO native AI-vs-HPC label. It can only weakly inform "do AI workloads fail more and why": the Anvil v3 slice (Jul 2022–May 2023) includes NVIDIA A100 GPU nodes, so GPU-pa
- **Caveats:** Root cause is not labeled — the diagnostic user tickets (~3,873) are withheld for privacy, so failure attribution must be inferred from exit codes + telemetry. Accounting and TACC Stats are joined only by timestamp (no shared job key), which introduces alignment noise. Schedulers differ across systems (TORQUE/Moab on Conte/Stampede vs Slurm on Anvil), so exit-code/job-state semantics are not unifo
- **Confidence:** high — existence confirmed via Purdue RC

The grid GWF traces inherit SWF field 11 "Status," a single integer completion code: 0=job failed, 1=job completed successfully, 5=job cancelled (2/3/4 for checkpointed partial executions). This gives a binary/ternary success-vs-fail-vs-cancel outcome label per job and nothing more — it does not identify why a job failed, gives no exit/return code, no error message, no node/hardware fault attribution. The distinction between failure, cancellation, and system termination is often imprecise and inherited from noisy source logs; some GWA traces leave Status unset/-1 (meaningless). The cloud VM traces (GWA-T-12 Bitbrains
- **Access:** Public / open. - **AI vs HPC:** HPC/grid-only (plus enterprise cloud VM telemetry) — contains NO AI/ML training or GPU workloads. ### HPAS (HPC Performance Anomaly Suite)
*aka:* peaclab HPAS synthetic anomaly generator; HPC Performance Anomaly Suite
*Org:* Boston University (PeacLab, Ayse Coskun group) + Sandia National Laboratories (Jim Brandt, Vitus Leung)  
*System:* Portable userspace synthetic anomaly injector; evaluated/used on Voltrino, Eclipse (Sandia), Cori (NERSC), and Volta clusters  
*Period:* Released 2019 (ICPP 2019 paper); actively reused through 2022-2025 downstream datasets  
*Scale:* Node-level userspace processes; injectable at arbitrary scale (any node count in a job); not a fixed corpus
- **Contents:** A suite of userspace synthetic anomaly generators reproducing common root causes of HPC performance variation. Eight generators across five subsystems: CPU contention (cpuoccupy), cache eviction/thrashing (cachecopy), memory bandwidth interference (membw), memory-intensive/OOM pressure (memeater), memory leak (memleak), network contention (netoccupy), shared-storage metadata-server contention (iometadata), and storage/bandwidth-server contention (iobandwidth). Each has runtime intensity knobs. No hardware/kernel/application modification required.
- **Failure info:** Critical distinction: HPAS is a LABEL-GENERATION ENGINE, not a failure/crash dataset. It does NOT expose real exit codes, job failures, or observed system faults. Instead it INJECTS a controlled, ground-truth-labeled performance anomaly of a KNOWN root-cause type into a running node — so the label is the anomaly class (which subsystem contention/leak) plus its intensity, established by construction because the experimenter chose what to inject and when. The "root cause" is fully known (it is the injected anomaly), which is exactly why it underpins supervised/diagnosis work. But note the anomalies are PERFORMANCE DEGRADATIONS (contention, leaks, slowdowns), NOT hard failures — no crashes, no 
- **Access:** Public, open source. GitHub: https://github.com/peaclab/HPAS (BSD 3-Clause). Paper (open PDF): https://www.bu.edu/peaclab/files/2019/06/ates_icpp19.pdf and https://megele.io/hpc-anomaly-suite-icpp19.pdf. DOI: https://doi.org/10.1145/3337821.3337907 (ICPP 2019).
- **Used by:** Ates et al., "HPAS: An HPC Performance Anomaly Suite for Reproducing Performance Variations," ICPP 2019 (originating paper). Reused as the anomaly/label engine in essentially all downstream BU/Sandia HPC anomaly work: Proctor; E2EWatch (Aksar et al.); ALBADross (Cluster 2022); Prodigy (dataset artif
- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-focused (traditional supercomputer performance anomalies), but methodologically a TOOL that applies to both HPC and AI clusters since its generators target generic subsystems (CPU, cache, memory bandwidth, network, shared storage) present in GPU/AI systems too. Directly relevant to the "why do w
- **Caveats:** Not a dataset — it is a generator, so "the dataset" only exists once you run it with a monitoring stack; the seed's framing as a dataset is imprecise. Anomalies are SYNTHETIC injections, not organically observed failures, so class balance, intensity, and timing are artifacts of the experiment, not real production distributions. Covers performance-variation root causes only (contention/leaks), NOT 
- **Confidence:** high — existence confirmed via the peacl

- **Access:** Public / open — Zenodo, CC BY 4.0. DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.3701440 (concept DOI 10.5281/zenodo.3701439). https://zenodo.org/record/3701440

- **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. It is CPU-cluster operational telemetry (CooLMUC/SuperMUC, MPI workloads) with injected-fault labels; there is no GPU/AI training-job content and no real failure logs. - **Caveats:** Fault labels are synthetic (FINJ-injected), so they represent controlled anomaly signatures rather than real hardware/software failures; label granularity is time-window level per node, not per-job with exit status. ### M100 dataset: time-aggregated data for anomaly detection
*aka:* M100 15-min aggregated; M100 anomaly-detection dataset; down-sampled M100 ExaData
*Org:* CINECA (with University of Bologna / DEI — Bartolini, Borghesi, Molan et al.)  
*System:* CINECA Marconi100 (M100), Tier-0 supercomputer  
*Period:* ~10 months of operation drawn from the larger M100 ExaData collection (data campaign spans 2020-2022; this curated release published Jan 2023)  
*Scale:* 980 compute nodes (each 2x IBM Power9 + 4x NVIDIA V100); one time-aggregated feature file per node/rack; 24.8 GB total (Parquet in per-rack tarballs)
- **Contents:** Per-node feature matrix, one row per 15-minute window. Hundreds of columns: IPMI/hardware telemetry summarized as aggregate statistics (average, std-dev, min, max) over the window — core loads, CPU/GPU temperatures, clock frequencies, memory read/write ops, CPU power consumption, fan speeds, GPU utilization — plus facility/infrastructure signals (liquid cooling, air conditioning, power supplies), workload-manager (Slurm) job statistics, and weather/ambient data. This is the down-sampled, ready-to-train derivative of the full M100 ExaData (raw ExaMon holistic monitoring, described in Nature Scientific Data s41597-023-02174-3).
- **Failure info:** Weak / coarse failure labeling. Each per-node file carries one extra label column of ANOMALY LABELS DERIVED FROM NAGIOS — the datacenter availability-monitoring system. The label marks each 15-min window per node as normal vs. anomalous, where "anomalous" corresponds to the node being in a failure/fault/maintenance state as flagged by Nagios (node down, unresponsive, or under maintenance). It is effectively a binary/categorical node-health state, NOT a rich fault taxonomy. It does NOT expose job exit codes, does NOT give a root-cause category (no distinction between hardware fault vs. thermal event vs. scheduled maintenance vs. network), and does NOT provide per-job failure attribution. - **AI vs HPC:** HPC-only. This is workload-agnostic node/infrastructure telemetry from a general-purpose Tier-0 HPC system, labeled with node-availability anomalies. It contains NO AI/ML-training-specific workload signals and cannot distinguish an AI job from any other job, so it cannot by itself answer "do AI work
- **Caveats:** Labels are only as good as Nagios: coarse, binary-ish, and conflate genuine failures with scheduled maintenance and monitoring gaps; anomalies are rare and the set is heavily imbalanced (which RUAD explicitly exploits). - **Caveats:** Enormous (49.9 TB) — practical use usually via monthly subsets or the per-node time-aggregated variant.
